30	pub enum BandLabels {
31	    Zpn,
32	    Frac,
33	    Both,
34	}
35	
36	impl BandLabels {
37	    /// Lowercase name used on the CLI and in the report header's
38	    /// `labels=` metadata.
39	    pub fn as_str(self) -> &'static str {
40	        match self {
41	            BandLabels::Zpn => "zpn",
42	            BandLabels::Frac => "frac",
43	            BandLabels::Both => "both",
44	        }
45	    }
46	
47	    /// Trimmed-stat row range label matching the style, so `n2`
48	    /// never appears in a frac-only table.
49	    pub fn trim_label(self) -> &'static str {
50	        match self {
51	            BandLabels::Frac => "min..0.99",
52	            BandLabels::Zpn | BandLabels::Both => "min..n2",
53	        }
54	    }
55	}
56	
57	/// One report band boundary: its CDF fraction plus its rendered
58	/// name in each label style.
59	pub struct Boundary {
60	    /// Fraction of samples at or below the boundary (CDF).
61	    pub pct: f64,
62	    /// nines/zeros / decile name (`z3`, `p50`, `n4`).
63	    pub zpn: String,
64	    /// Literal-fraction name, `_`-grouped (`0.001`, `0.999_9`).
65	    pub frac: String,
66	}
67	
68	impl Boundary {
69	    /// Row label for this boundary in the given style. min/max
70	    /// carry no fraction, so `both` prints them bare; elsewhere
71	    /// `both` pads zpn to its 3-char max so the fraction column
72	    /// aligns.
73	    pub fn label(&self, style: BandLabels) -> String {
74	        match style {
75	            BandLabels::Zpn => self.zpn.clone(),
76	            BandLabels::Frac => self.frac.clone(),
77	            BandLabels::Both if self.zpn == self.frac => self.zpn.clone(),
78	            BandLabels::Both => format!("{:<3} {}", self.zpn, self.frac),
79	        }
80	    }
81	}
82	
83	/// Group `digits` in threes with `_` so deep nines stay
84	/// countable at a glance, e.g. `"99999"` → `"999_99"`.
85	fn group3(digits: &str) -> String {
86	    let mut out = String::new();
87	    for (i, c) in digits.chars().enumerate() {
88	        if i > 0 && i % 3 == 0 {
89	            out.push('_');
90	        }
91	        out.push(c);
92	    }
93	    out
94	}
95	
96	/// The report's band boundaries, generated from
97	/// [`Z_DEPTH`]/[`N_DEPTH`]: familiar deciles in the body,
98	/// nines/zeros notation in both tails.
99	///

430	    }
431	
432	    // Whole-histogram and trimmed (min..n2, excluding every band
433	    // at or above n2 ≡ p99) summary values, rendered before the width
434	    // pass so the widths account for them — the untrimmed stdev
435	    // is often wider than any band mean and would otherwise
436	    // overflow its column, shifting its line right.
437	    let hist_mean = hist.mean() / PS_PER_NS;
438	    let hist_adj = (hist_mean - adj).max(0.0);
439	    let hist_mean_s = fmt_commas_f64(hist_mean, cfg.decimals);
440	    let hist_adj_s = fmt_commas_f64(hist_adj, cfg.decimals);
441	    let hist_stdev_s = fmt_commas_f64(hist.stdev() / PS_PER_NS, cfg.decimals);
442	
443	    let trim_count: u64 = band_count[..trim_bands].iter().sum();
444	    let trim = if trim_count > 0 {
445	        let trim_sum: u128 = band_sum[..trim_bands].iter().sum();
446	        let trim_mean = trim_sum as f64 / trim_count as f64 / PS_PER_NS;
447	        let trim_adj = (trim_mean - adj).max(0.0);
448	
449	        // Variance: walk histogram buckets, include only non-tail bands.
450	        let mut trim_var_sum = 0.0f64;
451	        let mut trim_var_count = 0u64;
452	        let mut cum = 0u64;
453	        for iv in hist.iter_recorded() {
454	            let value = iv.value_iterated_to();
455	            let count = iv.count_at_value();
456	            let mid_rank = (cum as f64 + count as f64 / 2.0) / sample_count as f64;
457	            let idx = bounds[1..]
458	                .iter()
459	                .position(|b| mid_rank < b.pct)
460	                .unwrap_or(n_bands - 1);
461	            if idx < trim_bands {
462	                let diff = value as f64 / PS_PER_NS - trim_mean;
463	                trim_var_sum += diff * diff * count as f64;
464	                trim_var_count += count;
465	            }
466	            cum += count;
467	        }
468	        let trim_stdev = if trim_var_count > 1 {
469	            (trim_var_sum / trim_var_count as f64).sqrt()
470	        } else {
471	            0.0
472	        };
473	
474	        Some((
475	            fmt_commas_f64(trim_mean, cfg.decimals),
476	            fmt_commas_f64(trim_adj, cfg.decimals),
477	            fmt_commas_f64(trim_stdev, cfg.decimals),
478	        ))

99	///
100	/// - `nK`/`zK` mark the boundary with a fraction 10^-K of
101	///   samples above (n) or below (z): n2 ≡ p99, n3 ≡ p99.9, …
102	///   and z2 ≡ p1, z3 ≡ p0.1, z4. "K nines" is standard
103	///   engineering shorthand for proportions near one
104	///   (nines = -log10(1-x)); zK is this project's mirror of it
105	///   for the fast tail.
106	/// - Deep bands populate only when the run has enough samples;
107	///   empty bands are skipped in the output.
108	pub fn boundaries() -> Vec<Boundary> {
109	    let mut v = vec![Boundary {
110	        pct: 0.0,
111	        zpn: "min".to_string(),
112	        frac: "min".to_string(),
113	    }];
114	    for k in (2..=Z_DEPTH).rev() {
115	        v.push(Boundary {
116	            pct: 10f64.powi(-k),
117	            zpn: format!("z{k}"),
118	            frac: format!("0.{}", group3(&("0".repeat(k as usize - 1) + "1"))),
119	        });
120	    }
121	    for d in 1..=9 {
122	        v.push(Boundary {
123	            pct: d as f64 / 10.0,
124	            zpn: format!("p{d}0"),
125	            frac: format!("0.{d}0"),
126	        });
127	    }
128	    for k in 2..=N_DEPTH {
129	        v.push(Boundary {
130	            pct: 1.0 - 10f64.powi(-k),
131	            zpn: format!("n{k}"),
132	            frac: format!("0.{}", group3(&"9".repeat(k as usize))),
133	        });
134	    }
135	    v.push(Boundary {
136	        pct: 1.0,
137	        zpn: "max".to_string(),
138	        frac: "max".to_string(),
139	    });
140	    v
141	}
142

144	mod tests {
145	    use super::*;
146	
147	    #[test]
148	    fn generated_boundaries_match_documented_ladder() {
149	        let b = boundaries();

151	        assert_eq!(
152	            zpn,
153	            [
154	                "min", "z4", "z3", "z2", "p10", "p20", "p30", "p40", "p50", "p60", "p70", "p80",
155	                "p90", "n2", "n3", "n4", "n5", "n6", "n7", "n8", "n9", "n10", "max",
156	            ]
157	        );
158	        let frac: Vec<&str> = b.iter().map(|x| x.frac.as_str()).collect();
159	        assert_eq!(
160	            frac,
161	            [
162	                "min",
163	                "0.000_1",
164	                "0.001",
165	                "0.01",
166	                "0.10",
167	                "0.20",
168	                "0.30",
169	                "0.40",
170	                "0.50",
171	                "0.60",
172	                "0.70",
173	                "0.80",
174	                "0.90",
175	                "0.99",
176	                "0.999",
177	                "0.999_9",
178	                "0.999_99",
179	                "0.999_999",
180	                "0.999_999_9",
181	                "0.999_999_99",
182	                "0.999_999_999",
183	                "0.999_999_999_9",
184	                "max",
185	            ]
186	        );
187	        // Endpoints exact; interior strictly increasing; the n2
188	        // trim anchor lands within a ulp of 0.99.
189	        assert_eq!(b[0].pct, 0.0);
190	        assert_eq!(b[b.len() - 1].pct, 1.0);
191	        assert!(b.windows(2).all(|w| w[0].pct < w[1].pct));
192	        let n2 = &b[13];
193	        assert!((n2.pct - 0.99).abs() < 1e-12, "n2 pct: {}", n2.pct);
194	    }
195	}
196	
```

320	/// per-band histogram, whole-histogram mean/stdev, and trimmed
321	/// mean/stdev (min..n2, excluding every band at or above n2 ≡ p99).
322	///
323	/// Each histogram row is one band, labeled by its **upper**
324	/// boundary — deciles in the body (`p10` … `p90`), nines/zeros in
325	/// the tails (`zK`/`nK` = fraction 10^-K of samples below/above
326	/// the boundary) — the lower boundary being the previous printed
327	/// row (empty bands are skipped). Label style comes from
328	/// `cfg.band_labels` and is recorded as `labels=` in the header
329	/// metadata so saved outputs are self-describing. The `adjusted`
330	/// columns subtract per-call apparatus overhead
331	/// (`cfg.overhead.per_call_ns(inner)`); the untrimmed `stdev` is the
332	/// hdrhistogram-native stdev, which includes the ms-scale outliers
333	/// in the tail band. Ends with `WARNING` lines flagging poisoned
334	/// stats when they apply — `suspended_s` comes from
335	/// [`run_adaptive`] (see [`warn_invalid`]).
336	pub fn print_report(
337	    name: &str,
338	    outer: u64,
339	    inner: u64,
340	    duration_s: f64,
341	    hist: &Histogram<u64>,
342	    cfg: &RunCfg,
343	    suspended_s: f64,
344	) {
345	    // Header line: bench name + logfmt-style metadata. `adj` is the
346	    // apparatus overhead subtracted from each sample downstream.
347	    let adj = cfg.overhead.per_call_ns(inner);
348	    let total = outer * inner;
349	    println!(
350	        "{name} [duration={:.1}s outer={} inner={} calls={} adj/call={}ns labels={}]:",
351	        duration_s,
352	        fmt_commas(outer),
353	        inner,
354	        fmt_commas(total),
355	        fmt_commas_f64(adj, 2),
356	        cfg.band_labels.as_str(),
357	    );
358	
359	    let bounds = bands::boundaries();
360	
361	    // Trimmed-stat row prefix labels, style-matched (see
362	    // [`BandLabels::trim_label`]).
363	    let trim_label = cfg.band_labels.trim_label();
364	    let mean_trim_label = format!("mean {trim_label}");
365	    let stdev_trim_label = format!("stdev {trim_label}");
366	
367	    // Trim anchor: bands at or above the n2 (p99) boundary are
368	    // the "tail" — excluded from the trimmed min..n2 stats no
369	    // matter how many finer tail bands subdivide them.
370	    #[allow(clippy::unwrap_used)]
371	    // OK: boundaries() always emits n2 (N_DEPTH >= 2)
372	    let trim_bands = bounds.iter().position(|b| b.zpn == "n2").unwrap();
373	
374	    let n_bands = bounds.len() - 1;
375	    let sample_count = hist.len();
376	
377	    // Accumulate per-band stats by walking recorded histogram buckets.
378	    // Each bucket is assigned to the band containing its midpoint rank.
379	    let mut band_first = vec![u64::MAX; n_bands];
380	    let mut band_last = vec![0u64; n_bands];
381	    let mut band_count = vec![0u64; n_bands];
382	    let mut band_sum = vec![0u128; n_bands];
383	
384	    let mut cumulative = 0u64;
385	    for iv in hist.iter_recorded() {
386	        let value = iv.value_iterated_to();
387	        let count = iv.count_at_value();
388	        let mid_rank = (cumulative as f64 + count as f64 / 2.0) / sample_count as f64;
389	        let idx = bounds[1..]
390	            .iter()
391	            .position(|b| mid_rank < b.pct)
392	            .unwrap_or(n_bands - 1);
393	        band_first[idx] = band_first[idx].min(value);
394	        band_last[idx] = band_last[idx].max(value);
395	        band_count[idx] += count;
396	        band_sum[idx] += value as u128 * count as u128;
397	        cumulative += count;
398	    }
399	
400	    // Build rendered rows: (label, first, last, range, count, mean, adj_mean).
401	    struct BandRow {
402	        label: String,
403	        first: String,
404	        last: String,
405	        range: String,
406	        count: String,
407	        mean: String,
408	        adj_mean: String,
409	    }
410	
411	    let mut rows: Vec<BandRow> = Vec::new();
412	    for i in 0..n_bands {
413	        if band_count[i] == 0 {
414	            continue;
415	        }
416	        let mean_ns = band_sum[i] as f64 / band_count[i] as f64 / PS_PER_NS;
417	        let adj_mean = (mean_ns - adj).max(0.0);
418	        rows.push(BandRow {
419	            label: bounds[i + 1].label(cfg.band_labels),
420	            first: fmt_commas_f64(band_first[i] as f64 / PS_PER_NS, cfg.decimals),
421	            last: fmt_commas_f64(band_last[i] as f64 / PS_PER_NS, cfg.decimals),
422	            range: fmt_commas_f64(
423	                (band_last[i] - band_first[i] + 1) as f64 / PS_PER_NS,
424	                cfg.decimals,
425	            ),
426	            count: fmt_commas(band_count[i]),
427	            mean: fmt_commas_f64(mean_ns, cfg.decimals),
428	            adj_mean: fmt_commas_f64(adj_mean, cfg.decimals),
429	        });
430	    }
431	
432	    // Whole-histogram and trimmed (min..n2, excluding every band
433	    // at or above n2 ≡ p99) summary values, rendered before the width
434	    // pass so the widths account for them — the untrimmed stdev
435	    // is often wider than any band mean and would otherwise
436	    // overflow its column, shifting its line right.
437	    let hist_mean = hist.mean() / PS_PER_NS;
438	    let hist_adj = (hist_mean - adj).max(0.0);
439	    let hist_mean_s = fmt_commas_f64(hist_mean, cfg.decimals);
```

> AGENT

I dug into how the report labels work. Here's what `min..n2` actually is and why the `min` looks orphaned.

## What the label means

The trimmed summary rows (`mean min..n2`, `stdev min..n2`) report stats over the **non-tail** samples — everything from the global minimum up to the p99 boundary (`n2` ≡ p99), excluding the top-1% tail bands (`n3 … max`). It's a **boundary-to-boundary range**:

- `min` = `boundaries()[0]` (`bands.rs:109-113`) — the floor of the whole histogram, pct 0.0
- `n2` = the p99 boundary — the upper cut of the trim (`harness.rs:372`)

Both `min` and `n2` are real entries in the boundary ladder, so as a *range of boundaries* the label is correct and stable.

## Why it looks wrong

Band **rows** are labeled by their **upper** boundary (`harness.rs:419`, `label: bounds[i + 1]`). So the row loop starts at `bounds[1]` — meaning `min` (`bounds[0]`) is **never** printed as a row label. The first visible row is `z4`/`z3`/whatever the smallest non-empty band is. So a reader scanning row names for `min` won't find it, and `min..n2` reads as referencing a row that isn't there.

Your instinct — "make `min` be what the first row displays" — would actually be **wrong** semantically: the trim genuinely includes all the sub-`z4` samples down to the true minimum, so labeling the floor `z4` (or whatever the first row is) would imply those samples are excluded, and it'd be *unstable* (changes with which bands are populated per run).

So the range endpoints are right; the friction is purely that `min` is a lower-boundary name in a table that only ever prints upper-boundary names.

I was thinking we should use whatever is the left most band-label of the first row?

> AGENT

Got it — so the trim label's lower end tracks the **first printed row's** band-label, giving e.g. `z4..n2` instead of `min..n2`. That's actually consistent with how the table reads (every row is named by its upper boundary, so "z4 row through n2 row" spans exactly the trimmed samples), and it kills the orphan `min` that never appears as a row.

Two consequences I want to confirm before I touch code:

**1. It becomes run-dependent.** The first non-empty band is whichever low band has data. The `z4` band (p0.01) needs ~10k samples to populate; with fewer samples the first row is `z3`, `p10`, etc. So the label would vary across runs — `z4..n2`, `p10..n2`, … — reflecting the actual lowest populated band. The underlying samples don't change (always min-value → p99); only the name of the floor does. I think that variation is fine (it's honest about what's populated), but it does make the label non-constant if anything parses it.

**2. `both` label style.** Right now `trim_label()` collapses `both` to the zpn form (`min..n2`, not `min..0.99`). The first row's *displayed* label in `both` style is the padded pair (e.g. `z4  0.000_1`), which would look bad inside a range. My plan: keep the current per-style convention — use the first row's `.zpn` for `zpn`/`both`, its `.frac` for `frac`. So:

- `zpn`  → `z4..n2`
- `both` → `z4..n2`
- `frac` → `0.000_1..0.99`

Does that match what you want — first row's simple style-name as the floor, upper end unchanged, and OK with it varying by run?

> DEVELOPER

yes, that matches what I want, but one question we tend to always choose n2 (0.99) for the right side. What if there is no n2/0.99 and in the most extereme case what if all runs are in one band, we should handle that case too, thoughts?

> AGENT

Good catch — the right side should be symmetric with the left, not hardcoded. Here's the rule I'd use:

**Trim label = span of actually-populated trimmed rows.** Within the trim range (bands below the tail, i.e. indices `0..trim_bands`):

- **lower** = style-name of the *first* non-empty band
- **upper** = style-name of the *last* non-empty band
- if first == last (only one populated trimmed band), **collapse to a single label** — no `..`

So:

| Case | Label (zpn / both) |
|---|---|
| Normal run | `z4..n2` |
| n2 band empty (spiky data, no samples in p90–p99) | `z4..p90` (or wherever the top populated trimmed band is) |
| All samples in one band | `p60` (single label, no range) |

The **computation is unchanged** — trimmed stats still sum every band below the `n2`/p99 cut. Only the *label* now names the real extent instead of asserting an `n2` (or `min`) row that may not exist. This is exactly the same "name the populated span, not the fixed boundary" principle you asked for on the left, applied to both ends.

Note this means the label essentially **never says `min` again** either — the first band is `z4`, so a full run reads `z4..n2`, a sparse one `p10..n2`, etc. The orphan is gone on both sides.

**Why last-populated instead of always-`n2`:** if the `n2` band is empty, there's genuinely no data between p90 and p99, so cutting "at p99" and "at p90" describe the identical sample set — naming the populated top is honest and matches the left-side logic. The trim *policy* (cut the tail at p99) still lives in the `print_report` doc comment and the `mean`/`stdev` prefix.

**One small call:** in the all-in-one-band case the trimmed stat equals the untrimmed `mean`/`stdev` (redundant rows). I'd still print it with the collapsed label rather than suppress it — simpler and consistent. Object if you'd rather suppress.

Sound right? If so I'll pick this up as a cycle (this is a `harness.rs` report change; `BandLabels::trim_label()` in `bands.rs` becomes unused and gets removed).

1	# Versioning
2	
3	How this project versions its commits and the running
4	artifact. The scheme — the cycle step-numbering and the
5	unique-per-commit aim — is generic and shared across projects:
6	this file is copied **verbatim**, with
7	[Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
8	covering each medium by conditional rather than per-project
9	edits.
10	
11	## Terms
12	
13	Three names, used as defined here across
14	[AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md),
15	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md), and the notes files:
16	
17	- **version** — the per-commit version (e.g. `0.3.0-5.3.0`),
18	  written with `-` in prose, ladders, chores, todo, and commit
19	  talk; its suffix encodes the cycle phase (see
20	  [Step numbering](#step-numbering)).
21	- **version-of-record** — the authoritative stored copy of the
22	  version, in the project's manifest (see
23	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record));
24	  a built or running artifact derives from it.
25	- **versioning** — the topic: this scheme as a whole.
26	
27	## Recording the version-of-record
28	
29	Where the version-of-record lives, how it's stored and
30	surfaced, and how often it changes — pick the case that fits
31	your medium:
32	
33	- **Manifest** — where the version-of-record is stored:
34	  - if Rust, `Cargo.toml` `[package].version`
35	  - if Python, `pyproject.toml` `[project].version` (or the
36	    committed config it's sourced from)
37	  - otherwise wherever the medium records it (a generic
38	    `version.toml`, a book's frontmatter, …) — add the case as
39	    needed
40	- **Notation** — how the `-` form is stored:
41	  - if the format allows `-` (TOML `version.toml`, `Cargo.toml`),
42	    store it verbatim
43	  - if it bars `-` (PEP 440's local segment, e.g. a Python
44	    project), remap to `+` — `0.3.0-5.3.0` → `0.3.0+5.3.0`: same
45	    version, just the stored spelling
46	- **Reporter** — how a built artifact surfaces the
47	  version-of-record:
48	  - if a CLI app, `<cli-app> -V`
49	  - if a TUI/GUI, add to Help/About or display on the title
50	- **Cadence** — how often to bump: see
51	  [Unique per commit](#unique-per-commit-preference-not-requirement);
52	  this project follows the per-commit preference.
53	
54	## Unique per commit (preference, not requirement)
55	
56	Our general notion is that the version-of-record should change
57	on **every commit**, so a built or running artifact identifies
58	the exact commit it came from.
59	
60	- This is a preference, **not** a hard requirement. A project
61	  following the cycle protocol may bump less often — once per
62	  cycle, only at release, and so on. Record the choice in
63	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
64	  if it differs.
65	- It is achievable because the cycle's versions (below) are
66	  **pre-assignable**, unlike the git SHA, which a commit cannot
67	  contain (see the cycle protocol's
68	  [Commits backfill](cycle-protocol.md#commits-backfill)).
69	
70	## Step numbering
71	
72	The cycle (Preparation -> Work -> Close-out; see
73	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md)) encodes each commit's
74	phase in the version suffix — the **final identifier `0` marks
75	a Preparation**:
76	
77	- `X.Y.Z-0` — Preparation
78	- `X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, … — Work commits
79	- `X.Y.Z` — Close-out (bare version, no suffix)
80	
81	**Preparation is optional.** A lightweight cycle — no ladder,
82	no setup commit — skips `-0` and starts at `-1` (its first
83	Work commit). The same holds at every level: a sub-cycle
84	needing no Preparation omits its `.0` (see Nesting). One that
85	grows a Preparation later adds the `0` step without
86	renumbering siblings.
87	
88	Disambiguation:
89	
90	- `-10` — Work commit #10 (final identifier `10`), not a
91	  Preparation.
92	- `-1.0` — Preparation of the `-1` sub-cycle (final
93	  identifier `0`).
94	
95	**Nesting.** Sub-cycles append another level, recursively:
96	
97	- `X.Y.Z-3.0` — Preparation of the `-3` sub-cycle
98	- `X.Y.Z-3.1`, `X.Y.Z-3.2` — its Work
99	- `X.Y.Z-3` — its Close-out
100	- `X.Y.Z-3.1.0` — Preparation of the `-3.1` sub-sub-cycle
101	
102	Bump the version-of-record at the start of each phase, so the
103	active phase is recorded — and, per the preference above,
104	every commit carries a distinct version.
105	
```

1	# Cycle protocol
2	
3	This protocol uses [Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form). It
4	contains instructions on how a commit cycle is accomplished.
5	
6	The artifact a cycle produces is whatever the bot generates from
7	the conversation — code, prose, an image, a song, a screenplay.
8	The steps below use a Rust crate as the running example (the
9	cargo cycle, `Cargo.toml` versioning); substitute your medium's
10	equivalents — this project's manifest is recorded in
11	[versioning.md](versioning.md).
12	
13	## Cycles
14	
15	A cycle has three phases:
16	
17	- **[Preparation](#preparation)** (`X.Y.Z-0`) — the cycle's
18	  first commit, when it needs setup (a lightweight cycle omits
19	  it and starts at `-1` — see
20	  [versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)). Sets up the
21	  cycle:
22	  - Bump the version-of-record to `X.Y.Z-0` (where it lives
23	    and the suffix scheme are project-specific — see
24	    [versioning.md](versioning.md)).
25	  - Pick up a `## Todo` item (typically the top-ranked,
26	    #1) into `## In Progress` (bold title + succinct problem
27	    statement + plan ladder).
28	  - Open the [chores section](#chores-sections).
29	- **[Work-N](#work-n)** (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …) — the
30	  commits that implement the change. As many as the change
31	  needs; each runs through the
32	  [per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow).
33	- **[Close-out](#close-out)** (bare `X.Y.Z`) — the cycle's
34	  last commit. Bookkeeping only:
35	  - Move the picked-up item to a one-line entry in
36	    `## Done`.
37	  - Move the `## In Progress` block into the
38	    [chores section](#chores-sections).
39	  - Optionally update `notes/README.md` if functionality
40	    changed.
41	
42	A cycle's commits are published to the project remote
43	either incrementally or as one batch at close-out; the
44	result must always be published at close-out. See
45	[Pushing](#pushing).
46	
47	**Sub-cycles.** When a Work commit's scope grows enough to
48	warrant its own ladder, it subdivides — `X.Y.Z-3.0`
49	Preparation, `X.Y.Z-3.1` / `X.Y.Z-3.2` Work, `X.Y.Z-3`
50	Close-out. The same three-phase shape applies recursively
51	at every depth. See [Numbering](#numbering) for the
52	suffix rule and [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)
53	for the local-ladder mechanics.
54	
55	## Chores sections
56	
57	A **chores section** is a `##` section in
58	`notes/chores/chores-NN.md` recording landed work. In
59	general, every commit that lands on the permanent branch
60	should have a reference to it on a `Commits:` list in a
61	chores file.
62	
63	The phrase **"Open" the chores section** means append a
64	`##` header to the current `notes/chores/chores-NN.md`
65	with the title it records (e.g. `## refactor: foo bar`),
66	followed by an **empty `Commits:`** line. The narrative
67	body stays empty until close-out (see [Close-out](#close-out));
68	the `Commits:` line is backfilled later, once the commit is
69	permanent (see [Commits backfill](#commits-backfill) below).
70	
71	Fuller chores conventions (content rules, header sync,
72	design subsection pattern, `Commits:` formatting) live in
73	AGENTS.md [Chores conventions](../AGENTS.md#chores-conventions).
74	
75	### Commits backfill
76	
77	A chores section's `Commits:` line cites the commit(s) it
78	records, by SHA — but a SHA isn't stable until the commit
79	lands on a **permanent branch** (`main`, or a long-lived
80	release/patch branch that won't be rewritten); a rebase or
81	squash rewrites it on the way. So:
82	
83	- A chores section is **opened with an empty `Commits:`** line.
84	- **Backfill once the commit is on a permanent branch**,
85	  where its SHA is final. A commit can't record its own SHA
86	  (that would change the hash), so the fill always lands one
87	  push later: **each push backfills the `Commits:` of the
88	  commits the previous push made permanent.** On a topic
89	  branch the sections instead wait until the branch lands —
90	  so no SHA is ever written that a later rebase could
91	  invalidate.
92	
93	Use `[[N]]` refs — several as `[[N]],[[M]]` only when one
94	section records multiple commits (a merge non-ff close-out) —
95	with the commit URL + 40-hex SHA in the file's `# References`
96	(format in AGENTS.md
97	[Chores commit references](../AGENTS.md#chores-commit-references)).
98	A section's `##` title matches its commit title, so a rare
99	deliberate rewrite of a permanent-branch commit re-syncs via
100	`git log --grep "<title>"`.
101	
102	The per-push cadence is a project choice, not dogma — a
103	**per-close-out** model (recording a cycle's SHAs at its
104	close-out) is equally valid. The one invariant: a recorded
105	SHA must be permanent.
106	
107	## Preparation
108	
109	The cycle's first commit (`X.Y.Z-0`), when the cycle needs
110	setup (a lightweight cycle omits it — see
111	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)):
112	
113	- **Bump the version-of-record** to `X.Y.Z-0`. Where it
114	  lives, the suffix scheme, and any derived files (a
115	  lockfile, a sourced manifest version) are
116	  project-specific — see [versioning.md](versioning.md).
117	- **Move a `## Todo` item** (if the cycle has one) into
118	  `## In Progress` and the todo item should have:
119	  - A **bold title line** — that will be the chores
120	    section header, minus the `## ` prefix.
121	  - A **succinct problem statement**; add if one is needed
122	  - A **plan ladder**.
123	- **Open the [chores section](#chores-sections)** —
124	  append a `##` header with the title it records — the
125	  cycle's anticipated close-out title.
126	
127	## Work-N
128	
129	The cycle's work commits (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …)
130	implement the change. As many as needed:
131	
132	- Each commit runs through the
133	  **[per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow)**.
134	- **Interim pushes** are optional (backup, progress
135	  visibility).
136	- Close-out is the only mandatory push (see
137	  [Pushing](#pushing)).
138	- **Subdivide into a sub-cycle** if a Work commit's
139	  scope grows enough (see
140	  [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)).
141	
142	## Close-out
143	
144	The cycle's last commit (bare `X.Y.Z`) does bookkeeping
145	only — the commit body describes that bookkeeping, not
146	what happens post-squash:
147	
148	- **Move the picked-up item** from `## In Progress` to a
149	  one-line entry (with a chores `[N]` ref) in `## Done`.
150	- **Move the `## In Progress` block into the
151	  [chores section](#chores-sections)** (the title-only
152	  placeholder opened during Preparation):
153	  - Cut the problem statement + plan ladder out of
154	    `## In Progress` (drop the bold title line — the
155	    chores `##` header already carries that string).
156	  - Paste under the chores `##` header.
157	  - Sync the chores header to the **final** commit title
158	    if the cycle's scope shifted; update every anchor
159	    back-reference.
160	  - Add an `### As-built ladder` listing the cycle's
161	    commits; optional `### Outcome` notes.
162	  - Replace `## In Progress` with
163	    `_No cycle currently in progress._`.
164	- **Update `notes/README.md`** if functionality changed
165	  (new flags, new subcommands, changed behavior).
166	
167	Whether to **squash** the cycle into one commit before the
168	publishing push, or push as-is, is decided at push time —
169	see [Pushing](#pushing).
170	
171	## Numbering
172	
173	Each commit's phase is encoded in the version suffix — `-0`
174	Preparation, `-1`/`-2`/… Work, bare `X.Y.Z` Close-out,
175	recursively for sub-cycles. The full scheme — disambiguation,
176	nesting, optional Preparation, the project's version-of-record
177	format, and the per-phase bump — lives in
178	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering), which is the
179	single source of truth for this repo's versioning.
180	
181	## Per-commit flow
182	
183	Every commit (Preparation, each Work commit, Close-out) goes
184	through:
185	
186	1. **Mark this commit `(current)`** as the first edit in
187	   `notes/todo.md > ## In Progress`.
188	2. **Do the work** (see [Iterative work](#iterative-work)
189	   for the loop-and-squash technique).
190	3. **Flip this commit `(current)` → `(done)`** in `## In
191	   Progress` — before the cargo cycle and the commit.
192	4. **Validate the artifact** — a medium-specific step, skip-able
193	   for notes-only commits, mandatory at close-out. For the Rust
194	   example the cargo cycle is:
195	   1. `cargo fmt`
196	   2. `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`
197	   3. `cargo test`
198	   4. `cargo install --path . --locked`
199	   5. (re-test if anything substantive changed)
200	5. **Work review.** Stop *before* writing any description;
201	   tell the user "ready to commit." The user reviews the
202	   changes and we iterate until complete.
203	6. **Write the commit description** — see
204	   [Commit description](#commit-description).
205	7. **Commit Description review.** Show the title + body
206	   and stop. The user reviews the description. Iterate.
207	8. **Commit.** `jj commit -m "title" -m "body" -R .` for
208	   the app repo, `-R .claude` for the bot repo (`-R` last
209	   keeps the verb visible):
210	
211	   ```
212	   jj commit -m \
213	   "<type>: <short description>" \
214	   -m "<intro paragraph>
215	
216	   - file1: gist
217	   - file2: gist
218	
219	   ochid: /.claude/<chid>" \
220	   -R .
221	   ```
222	
223	**Two overrides apply:**
224	
225	- **Deviation or question** — any time the work deviates
226	  from the agreed plan, or a question arises, stop and
227	  surface it; don't push through.
228	- **ESC-ESC** — the user can interrupt at any point to pull
229	  a review or question forward.
230	
231	## Commit description
232	
233	[Conventional Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/):
234	
235	```
236	<type>: <short description>
237	```
238	
239	Titles carry **no trailing `(<version>)` suffix**. The
240	version-of-record — where it lives and its bump cadence, see
241	[versioning.md](versioning.md) — is useful for confirming
242	you're running the version you're testing, not a per-commit
243	title marker. We think per-commit version-in-title is
244	unstable on large projects: many changes are in flight
245	simultaneously, and a
246	version is only stable once merged into the main repo — so
247	titles don't carry one.
248	
249	### Title
250	
251	- ≤50 chars total.
252	- Common types: `feat`, `fix`, `refactor`, `test`,
253	  `docs`, `chore`.
254	- Favor terse phrasings.
255	- **Distinct per step** — each of a cycle's commits gets its
256	  own descriptive title (no shared cycle title with a step
257	  marker). Share a greppable stem across the cycle's titles
258	  (e.g. `ring buffer`) so `git log --grep` collects them; the
259	  chores section header matches the close-out title. See
260	  AGENTS.md
261	  [Conventional-commit shape](../AGENTS.md#conventional-commit-shape-ladder--chores--commit).
262	
263	### Body
264	
265	[Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form) (intro + bullets),
266	wrap ≤72. Bullet content differs per repo:
267	
268	- **App-repo body**: file-by-file. One bullet per file
269	  changed (file plus a one-line gist). Sub-bullets for
270	  files with multiple distinct changes:
271	
272	  ```
273	  - path/to/file1
274	    - first distinct change
275	    - second distinct change, wrapping to next line
276	       with continuation indented 5
277	  ```
278	
279	  The file-by-file list is the source of truth for the
280	  cycle's mechanical change record. Chores carries the
281	  narrative + design, not a copy of it. Promote any
282	  "why" beyond one sentence to a chores `###` subsection.
283	
284	- **Session-repo (`.claude`) body**: bullets describe
285	  in-session activity rather than code changes.
286	
287	### Trailer
288	
289	`ochid:` as the last line of the body — see
290	[Cross-repo linking (ochid trailers)](../AGENTS.md#cross-repo-linking-ochid-trailers)
291	in AGENTS.md for the convention.
292	
293	For breaking changes, use the hyphenated `BREAKING-CHANGE:`
294	trailer key. `BREAKING CHANGE:` (with a space) is the only
295	space-separated key the Conventional Commits spec allows; the
296	hyphenated form is also valid and avoids the space ambiguity.
297	
298	## Reviewing changes
299	
300	Work review looks at the **uncommitted working-copy diff**,
301	on the way to commit. The user opens diffs in their
302	editor (Zed, VSCode); jj commands are for terminal:
303	
304	- `jj diff` — working-copy diff (uncommitted)
305	- `jj diff -r @-` — diff of the previous commit
306	- `jj diff --from <X> --to <Y>` — any two revisions
307	- `jj show -r <X>` — description + diff for one rev
308	
309	Don't `jj edit -r @-` to view a past commit — that marks
310	it mutable and shifts `@`; use `jj diff -r @-` or
311	`jj show -r @-`.
312	
313	See [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders) for the
314	close-out squash recipe and recovery; revset primitives
315	are in [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets).
316	
317	## Pushing
318	
319	### Policy
320	
321	Push is **discretionary** during the cycle (backup,
322	progress visibility) and **mandatory at close-out** —
323	the cycle's result must be published.
324	
325	**Approval is per-push.** Every push — any repo, any kind:
326	cycle push, interim backup, recovery/surgery force-push —
327	happens only after the user has reviewed the changes to be
328	published and explicitly approved that specific push.
329	Approval of a plan that *includes* a push does not authorize
330	the push itself; stop and ask again at the moment of pushing.
331	
332	**Default is interactive; an explicit scoped delegation waives
333	the gates.** The gates above — per-push approval, the
334	commit-description review (show title+body and stop), and the
335	hard stop after push/finalize — are the *interactive default*.
336	They yield when the user **explicitly** delegates a complete,
337	bounded task and authorizes carrying it through ("do all of X
338	and push each step, don't check in"). The bot then proceeds
339	through that task's commits and pushes without stopping, and
340	continues past each push to the next step. Conditions:
341	
342	- **Explicit grant** — never inferred from a task merely being
343	  well-scoped; the user's words must authorize unattended
344	  completion. "Commit and push" (or "then push") names the
345	  destination, not a waiver: it authorizes the push *after*
346	  the normal work review and description review, not skipping
347	  them. Only wording that explicitly waives the stops ("don't
348	  check in", "no need to review", "carry it through
349	  unattended") waives them.
350	- **Bounded goal** — covers the named task only; does not carry
351	  to the next task or a vaguer follow-on.
352	- **Destructive ops still pause** — delegation covers the task's
353	  ordinary commits and pushes; it does *not* pre-authorize a
354	  genuinely irreversible action (force-push over published
355	  history, history rewrite, deleting a remote branch). Those can
356	  permanently destroy work and aren't a normal cycle step, so the
357	  bot flags one before acting. An ordinary delegated cycle never
358	  reaches this.
359	- **Still transparent** — report each commit/push as it lands
360	  (title + outcome) so the user can catch up.
361	- **When in doubt, ask** — ambiguous authorization falls back to
362	  per-push approval.
363	
364	### Shape at close-out push
365	
366	At close-out the cycle's *work* is done; its *published
367	shape* is the remaining choice, made at push time. Surface
368	the options and get user approval before pushing — once on
369	the target, changing shape is a remote rewrite (force-push,
370	needs approval), so choose deliberately.
371	
372	- **Squash to one commit** — single entry on the target.
373	  Right for straightforward changes where the Work-N is
374	  focused on one or two files.
375	- **Merge non-ff** *(current default)* — `main` gains a
376	  merge commit (`X.Y.Z`); cycle commits stay reachable via
377	  two parents. `jj log -r ..@ -n <N>` shows the trapezoidal
378	  shape. See [Merge non-ff recipe](#merge-non-ff-recipe)
379	  for the full setup sequence.
380	- **Keep separate** — one commit per cycle entry on
381	  `main`. Use when the decomposition itself is
382	  informative. Each chores section keeps its own header /
383	  `Commits:` ref; no consolidation churn.
384	
385	Set up squash/merge before invoking `vc-x1 push`; use
386	`jj git push` directly for non-standard shapes.
387	
388	### Merge non-ff recipe
389	
390	Setting up a [Merge non-ff](#shape-at-close-out-push)
391	close-out is a fixed sequence. `<closeout>` is the cycle's
392	close-out commit, `<prev>` the previous cycle's close-out
393	(the current `main` tip), `<work-tip>` the cycle's last
394	Work commit:
395	
396	1. **Rebase the close-out into a merge** — `jj rebase -r
397	   <closeout> --onto <prev> --onto <work-tip>`.
398	   - `-r <closeout>` keeps the `<closeout>` commit in place.
399	   - `--onto <prev>` becomes its first parent (trunk).
400	   - `--onto <work-tip>` becomes the second parent.
401	
402	   Together these make `<closeout>` a merge of
403	   `<prev>` + `<work-tip>`, forming a trapezoidal commit.
404	2. **Use `jj new <merge>`** to add an empty `@` above the
405	   merge. The rebase left `@` *on* the now-content-bearing
406	   merge, which git/IDE diff views show as uncommitted;
407	   `jj new` restores the clean empty `@` on top.
408	3. **Push** — `jj git push --bookmark main -R .`.
409	
410	**Post-hoc caveat.** If the cycle was already pushed
411	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) its commits are
412	immutable: the rebase needs `--ignore-immutable` and the
413	push force-updates `main`.
414	The standard sequence assumes the merge is set up *before*
415	the close-out push.
416	
417	### vc-x1 push wrapper
418	
419	`vc-x1 push <bookmark>` wraps per-push mechanics. See
420	`vc-x1 push --help` for current flags.
421	
422	**Current limitation**: only fully supports the
423	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) shape; other
424	shapes need manual jj steps. Improvements tracked /
425	planned:
426	
427	- N:1 code↔bot for Merge non-ff (`## Todo` entry P1).
428	- Symmetric squash (planned, to be captured in `-2`).
429	- Per-repo bookmark names (planned).
430	
431	### .claude cadence
432	
433	**Cadence**: one push = one `.claude` commit, paired
434	with every code commit in that push.
435	
436	The `.claude` working copy accumulates session data
437	across the cycle; its change ID stays stable across
438	snapshots, `jj describe`, and the finalize commit, so
439	code-side `ochid:` trailers resolve.
440	
441	`.claude` is a linear journal — all session work lives
442	on `main`, regardless of the app-side bookmark. **Do not
443	create or maintain `.claude` bookmarks that mirror
444	app-side branches** — risks the bot steering session
445	pushes to the wrong remote ref.
446	
447	Ending a session: if the user runs `/exit` there will be
448	session information created, which we don't worry about.
449	The user can close the terminal instead and `@` will
450	remain empty.
451	
452	### Bot communication at the reviews
453	
454	Use plain prose — no insider jargon ("Gate N signal",
455	"Checkpoint N", etc.):
456	
457	- **At Work review** — summarize what changed and stop.
458	  "Work complete. Please review."
459	- **At Commit Description review** — present `$TITLE`
460	  and `$BODY` explicitly; ask permission to commit/push.
461	  Don't spell out the full `vc-x1 push ... --title ...
462	  --body ...` invocation by default.
463	- **At Post close-out review** — surface the shape
464	  options (squash / merge / keep) and the push target;
465	  wait for the user's choice before any `jj squash` /
466	  `jj rebase` / `jj git push` invocation.
467	
468	### After push or finalize: stop and wait
469	
470	After you **push** (cross the remote boundary) or launch
471	**finalize** on the `.claude` repo (`vc-x1 finalize --repo
472	.claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach`) — by
473	hand or via the `vc-x1 push` wrapper — you **MUST NEVER**
474	proceed (next step, edit, tool call, text output) until the
475	user explicitly directs you to continue. **Even when the next
476	step seems obvious — wait.** Treat push-or-finalize as a hard
477	stop for the whole turn.
478	
479	`vc-x1 push` performs both as its tail stages — `push-app`
480	then the detached `vc-x1 finalize` on `.claude` — so there is
481	no gap left to speak in once the push is invoked. Put **all**
482	closing words *before* invoking it. The harness rejects an empty
483	turn, so it may force a visible token after the tool returns; if
484	so, emit a bare acknowledgment only (e.g. "landed") — never a
485	summary, verification, or next-step offer.
486	
487	Why (`.claude` is the live journal finalize snapshots →
488	squashes → pushes):
489	
490	- **`--detach`**: lets the tool return immediately so the
491	  harness can keep flushing the *trailing* session-data — the
492	  tool call's own transcript and any words emitted *before*
493	  the invoke — into the snapshot.
494	- **`--delay 10`**: gives the harness time to complete that
495	  flush before the snapshot is taken. It is **not** time for
496	  the bot to do more work.
497	- **silence**: the bot must not summarize or perform any work
498	  after the tool returns. The harness may force a visible token
499	  (it rejects an empty turn) — keep it to a bare acknowledgment,
500	  never a summary or a next step.
501	
502	**Known slip**: the bot has emitted a summary after launching
503	finalize. There is no "harmless" closing line — if it's worth
504	saying, say it *before* the invoke.
505	
506	### Recovery
507	
508	- **If push exits before `finalize-claude`** (e.g.
509	  failure between `push-app` and `finalize-claude`), run
510	  finalize by hand:
511	
512	  ```
513	  vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach --log /tmp/vc-x1-finalize.log
514	  ```
515	
516	- **Run finalize again if `@` is non-empty** after the
517	  finalize's squash-and-commit (also desirable after
518	  extra activity by the bot's agents).
519	  - Why: finalize snapshots `.claude` at `--delay`, but the
520	    bot keeps writing session data while it runs — the tool's
521	    own invocation plus the bot's closing response land
522	    *after* the snapshot.
523	  - Safe to repeat: bot session data is append-only, so a
524	    re-run never conflicts or overwrites. (This could
525	    change; it is not under the user's control.)
526	  - Fix: run `vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push
527	    <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach` another time to capture
528	    that tail; one extra pass is generally enough.
529	  - No guarantees: events outside the bot's control can leave
530	    `@` non-empty — e.g. the bot's back end may decide to
531	    squash/consolidate session data, which can take minutes
532	    and land after the snapshot. The remedy is the same: just
533	    run finalize again. This is why a single pass is never
534	    guaranteed to leave `@` empty.
535	- **Clear push's saved state** after any out-of-band
536	  recovery — `rm .vc-x1/push-state.toml` or `vc-x1 push
537	  <bookmark> --restart` — otherwise push resumes from a
538	  stale stage.
539	- **Late code-repo tweak after `push-app` succeeded**
540	  (e.g. updating AGENTS.md or memory) requires `jj
541	  squash --ignore-immutable` and a re-push; that is a
542	  remote rewrite and needs explicit approval like any
543	  push.
544	
545	## Iterative work
546	
547	When work for a single commit (the **target**) benefits
548	from incremental review, loop:
549	
550	1. `jj new -R .` — fresh empty `@` on top of the target.
551	2. Make the next round of changes.
552	3. User reviews the round (see
553	   [Reviewing changes](#reviewing-changes)).
554	4. `jj squash -R .` folds into the target and creates a
555	   new empty `@`.
556	5. If not done, go to step 2.
557	
558	Same jj mechanics as a
559	[sub-cycle ladder](#sub-cycle-ladders), but at
560	single-commit scope — the version
561	doesn't change.
562	
563	## Sub-cycle ladders
564	
565	When a Work commit subdivides into a sub-cycle (see
566	[Numbering](#numbering) for suffix nesting), its Work
567	commits typically live as a local jj `@` chain and
568	**collapse into the sub-cycle's Close-out** before the
569	parent cycle continues. Ladder commits are scratch —
570	for review and bisection only.
571	
572	### Per-Work-commit contract within a ladder
573	
574	For each Work commit in the ladder:
575	
576	1. `jj new -R .` — create a fresh empty `@`.
577	2. Do the commit's work.
578	3. Run the fast validation (Rust example: `cargo test
579	   --bins`). **Non-negotiable** — for code, build and clippy
580	   alone miss regressions until a later commit runs the full
581	   suite, raising bisection cost.
582	4. `jj describe -m "..." -m "..." -R .` — working title
583	   only; the sub-cycle Close-out collects everything
584	   into one final commit.
585	
586	### Navigating the ladder
587	
588	Common moves:
589	
590	- `jj log -r '<base>::' -R .` — see the whole ladder
591	  from its base.
592	- `jj edit -r <prefix> -R .` — jump `@` to any ladder
593	  commit by chid prefix; useful for bisection.
594	- `jj edit @-- -R .` — quick-jump back two commits.
595	- `jj diff -r <chid> -R .` — review one commit in
596	  isolation.
597	
598	Modifications to any ladder commit rewrite it in place;
599	descendants auto-rebase.
600	
601	### Close-out: squash the ladder
602	
603	When all ladder Work commits are done and tests pass:
604	
605	```
606	jj squash --from "<base>..@-" --into @ -u -R .
607	```
608	
609	`<base>` is the parent of the first ladder commit; `-u`
610	keeps `@`'s description and discards the sources'.
611	After squash, history is linear: `<base> → @`;
612	intermediate commits are auto-abandoned.
613	
614	Then `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` as for any other commit.
615	
616	For N = 1 the squash is a no-op (`<base>..@-` is empty
617	when `@-` is `<base>`); push the single commit directly.
618	
619	### Recovery
620	
621	If a ladder commit goes wrong, back out without losing
622	prior commits:
623	
624	- **Discard the current commit.** `jj abandon @ -R .`
625	  drops it; you get a fresh empty `@` on the same
626	  parent.
627	- **Edit an earlier commit.** `jj edit -r <chid> -R .`,
628	  make corrections, then `jj edit -r <last-ladder-chid>`
629	  to return. Descendants auto-rebase.
630	- **Discard the entire ladder.** `jj op log -R .` shows
631	  the op history; `jj op restore <op-id> -R .` reverts
632	  to that point. Full undo — removes *all* ladder work
633	  after the chosen op. Use only to start over.
634	
635	# References
636	
637	- [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets) — revset primitives
638	  (chid/cid, `@`/`@-`/`@+`, `..`/`::` ranges, prefix matching).
639	- [`substep-test.sh`](substep-test.sh) — script that
640	  scaffolds a 4-revision ladder under `/tmp/substep-test`
641	  for squash-recipe experiments.
642	- The per-commit `cargo test --bins` gate exists because a
643	  regression introduced in an early ladder commit can go
644	  uncaught until a later commit runs the full suite, raising
645	  bisection cost.
646	
```

266	## feat: config file + pin profiles
267	
268	Commits: [[19]]
269	
270	Defaults and named pin profiles now come from a layered config —
271	built-in defaults < `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/iiac-perf/config.toml` <
272	project-local `iiac-perf.toml` (cwd, no upward walk) < CLI. The
273	config is where the report options that landed the prior cycle
274	(`--band-labels`, `--decimals`) and `--duration` get their
275	persisted defaults, so common invocations stop repeating flags.
276	
277	- `src/config.rs`: a `RawConfig` deserialize target (serde +
278	  toml, `deny_unknown_fields` so a typo is caught), overlaid
279	  XDG-then-local into a validated `Config` of `Option` scalars
280	  (`None` = "no opinion, use built-in") plus a flat profiles
281	  map. A present-but-malformed file is fatal, not a silent
282	  fallback.
283	- `--pin <name>` resolves a `[profiles]` entry to its core spec
284	  before parsing (`smt = "0,12"` ⇒ `--pin smt` is `--pin 0,12`);
285	  a non-profile value parses as raw cores, so existing specs are
286	  unaffected.
287	- `main` resolves precedence per field (`cli.x.or(config.x)
288	  .unwrap_or(built_in)`); `--band-labels`/`--decimals` dropped
289	  their clap `default_value_t` and became `Option` so
290	  CLI-vs-config-vs-default is distinguishable.
291	- Banner gains a `config` line naming the loaded files (or
292	  `none (built-in defaults)`).
293	- `iiac-perf.toml.example`: a ready-to-copy sample at every key's
294	  built-in default, documenting each key's meaning and possible
295	  values; `.example` suffix so it is never auto-loaded from cwd.
296	  README's Config file section links it.
297	
298	### Why band_labels maps by string, not serde on the enum
299	
300	`BandLabels` lives in `bands.rs` as a `clap::ValueEnum`; the
301	config deserializes it as a plain string and maps it in
302	`validate`, keeping serde out of `bands.rs`. The mapping is the
303	same three names clap already accepts, and a bad value gets a
304	config-specific error (`"nope" is not one of zpn, frac, both`)
305	rather than a serde enum-variant message.
306	
307	## refactor: drop zcr raw/spin bench tiers
308	
309	Commits:
310	
311	zc-ring-x1 simplified its API down to a single `reserve_slot_with`
312	per endpoint, removing `reserve_slot` and `reserve_slot_spin`. The
313	`zcr-raw-*` and `zcr-spin-*` benches only existed to measure those
314	two now-gone tiers, so they go with them; the `zcr-with-*` pair is
315	the whole zcr family now.
316	
317	- Removed `zcr_raw_1t/2t.rs` + `zcr_spin_1t/2t.rs` and their
318	  `mod.rs` `pub mod` + `REGISTRY` entries; `zcr_common.rs` stays
319	  (shared by the `with` benches).
320	- Reworded the `zcr-with-*` doc comments that cross-referenced the
321	  deleted benches, and dropped the four rows + "three API tiers"
322	  phrasing from README's results table.
323	- Switched the `zc-ring-x1` dep from the local `../zc-ring-x1`
324	  path to the GitHub git source (Cargo.lock pins the resolved
325	  commit); trivially revertible to path.
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/src/harness.rs",
  "old_string": "        cumulative += count;\n    }\n\n    // Build rendered rows: (label, first, last, range, count, mean, adj_mean).",
  "new_string": "        cumulative += count;\n    }\n\n    // Trimmed-stat range label: the first..last *populated* band\n    // within the trim range (indices `0..trim_bands`), each named by\n    // its upper boundary like the rows above. This tracks the real\n    // extent instead of asserting a `min`/`n2` row that may be empty\n    // — `min` is never a row (rows use upper boundaries), and the n2\n    // band is empty when nothing lands in p90..p99. Collapses to one\n    // name when a single trimmed band holds all the data; empty when\n    // none do (only when there are no samples, so `trim` is None and\n    // the label goes unused).\n    let trim_first = (0..trim_bands).find(|&i| band_count[i] > 0);\n    let trim_last = (0..trim_bands).rev().find(|&i| band_count[i] > 0);\n    let trim_range = match (trim_first, trim_last) {\n        (Some(f), Some(l)) if f == l => bounds[f + 1].trim_name(cfg.band_labels).to_string(),\n        (Some(f), Some(l)) => format!(\n            \"{}..{}\",\n            bounds[f + 1].trim_name(cfg.band_labels),\n            bounds[l + 1].trim_name(cfg.band_labels),\n        ),\n        _ => String::new(),\n    };\n    let mean_trim_label = format!(\"mean {trim_range}\");\n    let stdev_trim_label = format!(\"stdev {trim_range}\");\n\n    // Build rendered rows: (label, first, last, range, count, mean, adj_mean)."
}

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/src/harness.rs",
  "old_string": "/// Print the full bench report: header line (logfmt-style metadata),",
  "new_string": "/// Build the trimmed-stat range label from the populated bands\n/// below the n2 ≡ p99 tail cut.\n///\n/// - Names the first..last populated band in `band_count[..trim_bands]`\n///   by its **upper** boundary (`bounds[i + 1]`), matching the row\n///   labels — so the label tracks the real extent of the trimmed\n///   data rather than asserting a `min` row (never printed — rows use\n///   upper boundaries) or an `n2` band that can be empty.\n/// - Collapses to a single name when one band holds all the trimmed\n///   data (`p60`, not `p60..p60`).\n/// - Empty string when no trimmed band is populated — only with no\n///   samples at all, where the caller's `trim` is `None` and the\n///   label goes unused.\nfn trim_range_label(\n    bounds: &[bands::Boundary],\n    band_count: &[u64],\n    trim_bands: usize,\n    style: BandLabels,\n) -> String {\n    let first = (0..trim_bands).find(|&i| band_count[i] > 0);\n    let last = (0..trim_bands).rev().find(|&i| band_count[i] > 0);\n    match (first, last) {\n        (Some(f), Some(l)) if f == l => bounds[f + 1].trim_name(style).to_string(),\n        (Some(f), Some(l)) => format!(\n            \"{}..{}\",\n            bounds[f + 1].trim_name(style),\n            bounds[l + 1].trim_name(style),\n        ),\n        _ => String::new(),\n    }\n}\n\n/// Print the full bench report: header line (logfmt-style metadata),"
}

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/src/harness.rs",
  "old_string": "        // Saturates instead of overflowing on absurd inputs.\n        assert_eq!(round_elapsed_ps(u128::MAX - 1, 1), u64::MAX);\n    }\n}",
  "new_string": "        // Saturates instead of overflowing on absurd inputs.\n        assert_eq!(round_elapsed_ps(u128::MAX - 1, 1), u64::MAX);\n    }\n\n    /// A `band_count` vec (len = n_bands) with the given band\n    /// indices marked populated.\n    fn counts(n_bands: usize, populated: &[usize]) -> Vec<u64> {\n        let mut c = vec![0u64; n_bands];\n        for &i in populated {\n            c[i] = 1;\n        }\n        c\n    }\n\n    #[test]\n    fn trim_range_label_spans_populated_bands() {\n        let bounds = bands::boundaries();\n        let n_bands = bounds.len() - 1;\n        // OK: boundaries() always emits n2 (N_DEPTH >= 2)\n        let trim_bands = bounds.iter().position(|b| b.zpn == \"n2\").unwrap();\n\n        // Full range: first band (label z4) through the n2 band.\n        let c = counts(n_bands, &[0, 5, trim_bands - 1]);\n        assert_eq!(trim_range_label(&bounds, &c, trim_bands, BandLabels::Zpn), \"z4..n2\");\n        assert_eq!(trim_range_label(&bounds, &c, trim_bands, BandLabels::Both), \"z4..n2\");\n        assert_eq!(\n            trim_range_label(&bounds, &c, trim_bands, BandLabels::Frac),\n            \"0.000_1..0.99\"\n        );\n\n        // n2 band empty: upper end is the last populated band (p90).\n        let c = counts(n_bands, &[0, 11]);\n        assert_eq!(trim_range_label(&bounds, &c, trim_bands, BandLabels::Zpn), \"z4..p90\");\n\n        // One populated band collapses to a single name.\n        let c = counts(n_bands, &[8]);\n        assert_eq!(trim_range_label(&bounds, &c, trim_bands, BandLabels::Zpn), \"p60\");\n\n        // No populated trimmed band yields an empty label (unused).\n        let c = counts(n_bands, &[]);\n        assert_eq!(trim_range_label(&bounds, &c, trim_bands, BandLabels::Zpn), \"\");\n    }\n}"
}

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/notes/chores/chores-04.md",
  "old_string": "## fix: trim label spans populated bands\n\nCommits:\n\n# References",
  "new_string": "## fix: trim label spans populated bands\n\nCommits:\n\nThe trimmed-stat summary rows (`mean`/`stdev`) were labeled with a\nfixed `min..n2`. But band rows are named by their **upper** boundary,\nso `min` never prints as a row, and the `n2` band can be empty (spiky\ndata with nothing in p90..p99) — the label asserted two rows that may\nnot exist. Now both ends are derived from the actual populated bands\nwithin the trim range (indices `0..trim_bands`, the bands below the\nn2 ≡ p99 tail cut), each named by its upper boundary like the rows\nabove.\n\n- The lower end is the first populated non-tail band, the upper end\n  the last; a run with the low tail empty reads `p20..n2`, one with\n  the `n2` band empty reads `..p90`, and a single populated band\n  collapses to one name (`p60`, not `p60..p60`).\n- The trim *computation* is unchanged — still every band below the\n  n2 cut. Only the label tracks the real extent instead of the fixed\n  boundary pair.\n- `BandLabels::trim_label` (the old static `min..n2` / `min..0.99`)\n  is gone; a new `Boundary::trim_name` returns the bare style-name\n  (`both` reuses `zpn`, as the trim label always did), and\n  `harness::trim_range_label` assembles the range — unit-tested for\n  the full-range, empty-`n2`, single-band, and no-samples cases.\n\n# References"
}

195	[survival function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survival_function)
196	/ CCDF tail fraction). The slow tail subdivides down to `n10`, the
197	fast tail only to `z4` — a latency distribution is floored below
198	(nothing beats the fast path) and open above. A band only prints
199	when it has samples, so deep tail rows appear as run length earns
200	them (populating `n10` takes ~1e10 calls). Each row shows first,
201	last, range (`last - first + 1`), count, mean, and adjusted mean.
202	The trimmed `min..n2` rows exclude every band at or above
203	`n2` (p99).
204	
205	The full boundary ladder across its range (label styles per
206	`--band-labels`). The ladder is generated by
207	[`src/bands.rs`](src/bands.rs) — the single source of truth for
208	boundaries and labels — and this table is pinned by that module's
209	unit test, so code and docs can't silently drift:
210	
211	| zpn       | frac              | ≡ percentile    | tail fraction |
212	|-----------|-------------------|-----------------|---------------|
213	| `z4`      | `0.000_1`         | p0.01           | 1e-4 below    |
214	| `z3`      | `0.001`           | p0.1            | 1e-3 below    |
215	| `z2`      | `0.01`            | p1              | 1e-2 below    |
216	| `p10`–`p90` | `0.10`–`0.90`   | deciles         | —             |
217	| `n2`      | `0.99`            | p99             | 1e-2 above    |
218	| `n3`      | `0.999`           | p99.9           | 1e-3 above    |
219	| `n4`      | `0.999_9`         | p99.99          | 1e-4 above    |
220	| `n5`      | `0.999_99`        | p99.999         | 1e-5 above    |
221	| `n6`      | `0.999_999`       | p99.9999        | 1e-6 above    |
222	| `n7`      | `0.999_999_9`     | p99.99999       | 1e-7 above    |
223	| `n8`      | `0.999_999_99`    | p99.999999      | 1e-8 above    |
224	| `n9`      | `0.999_999_999`   | p99.9999999     | 1e-9 above    |
225	| `n10`     | `0.999_999_999_9` | p99.99999999    | 1e-10 above   |
226	
227	The adjusted column subtracts apparatus overhead
228	(`framing_per_sample / inner + loop_per_iter`), calibrated once at
229	startup via a two-point fit on an empty bench. The startup banner
230	reports `cal pin` (calibration pinning) and `bench pin` (per-bench
231	thread pool) separately.
232	
233	Runs inhibit system sleep by default (see `--no-inhibit`), so the
234	flags below mainly matter for uninhibited runs. A report may end
235	with `WARNING` lines (printed last so they can't scroll out of
236	mind) flagging that `max` and the untrimmed mean/stdev are
237	poisoned. The few inflated samples land in the
238	extreme tail band, so percentile boundaries, the bands below the
239	tail, and the trimmed `min..n2` rows remain usable:
240	
241	- **system suspended** — the run spanned a system suspend,
242	  detected by `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` vs `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` elapsed
243	  divergence. A mid-sample suspend inflates that one sample by
244	  the whole sleep gap.
```

> AGENT

Work complete and validated (fmt + clippy clean, 23 tests pass, installs as `0.17.0`, verified live — a real `min-now` run now shows `mean p20..n2` with no orphaned `min`). Ready to commit. Please review.

**What changed:**

*Code*
- `src/bands.rs` — dropped `BandLabels::trim_label()` (the static `min..0.99`/`min..n2`); added `Boundary::trim_name(style)` returning the bare style-name (`both` reuses `zpn`).
- `src/harness.rs` — new `trim_range_label()` helper derives the label from the first/last **populated** band within the trim range (`0..trim_bands`), named by upper boundary like the rows; collapses to one name for a single band; empty when none. Wired into `print_report`; updated three doc/comment references off the old `min..n2`. Added `trim_range_label_spans_populated_bands` unit test covering full-range, empty-`n2`, single-band, and no-samples.

*Version + notes*
- `Cargo.toml`/`Cargo.lock` → `0.17.0`.
- `notes/todo.md` — In Progress cleared; Done entry `fix: trim label spans populated bands [[50]]` + ref.
- `notes/chores/chores-04.md` — filled this cycle's section; **backfilled** the `drop zcr` section's `Commits:` with `[[20]]` (c3d19d9, now permanent on main).
- `README.md` — updated the two prose descriptions of the trimmed rows.

**One thing to flag:** README's example-output blocks (lines ~346–415) still show `mean min-p99`/`stdev min-p99` — an even older label that predates both my change *and* the `min..n2` it replaced. Those are captured real-run snapshots, so refreshing them means re-running the benches. I left them out of scope for this fix. Want me to (a) leave them, (b) hand-edit just the labels in those snapshots, or (c) open a Todo to regenerate the example outputs?

1	//! Free-form measurement probe: a named, single-writer histogram
2	//! of sample durations in nanoseconds.
3	//!
4	//! The caller captures endpoints with any time source (typically
5	//! `minstant::Instant::now()`) and feeds the elapsed delta to
6	//! [`Probe::record`]. At report time [`Probe::report`] renders a
7	//! band-table summary under the enclosing bench's report, using
8	//! the same percentile boundaries so columns line up visually.
9	
10	use hdrhistogram::Histogram;
11	
12	use crate::harness::{fmt_commas, fmt_commas_f64};
13	
14	const BOUNDARY_PCTS: &[f64] = &[
15	    0.0, 0.01, 0.10, 0.20, 0.30, 0.40, 0.50, 0.60, 0.70, 0.80, 0.90, 0.99, 1.0,
16	];
17	const BOUNDARY_NAMES: &[&str] = &[
18	    "min", "p1", "p10", "p20", "p30", "p40", "p50", "p60", "p70", "p80", "p90", "p99", "max",
19	];
20	
21	/// A named, single-writer nanosecond histogram. Not `Sync`;
22	/// cross-thread *sharing* is out of scope. `Send` so probes can
23	/// be moved between threads (e.g. returned via a
24	/// `JoinHandle<Probe>` on shutdown).
25	pub struct Probe {
26	    name: String,
27	    hist: Histogram<u64>,
28	}
29	
30	impl Probe {
31	    /// Create an empty probe. Histogram bounds (1 ns — 60 s,
32	    /// 3 significant figures) mirror the harness.
33	    ///
34	    /// Exits the process (code 1) if the hardware tick counter
35	    /// isn't usable — see [`crate::ticks::require_ok`].
36	    pub fn new(name: &str) -> Self {
37	        crate::ticks::require_ok();
38	        Self {
39	            name: name.to_string(),
40	            hist: Histogram::<u64>::new_with_bounds(1, 60_000_000_000, 3).unwrap(),
41	        }
42	    }
43	
44	    /// Record a single sample. Values of 0 are clamped to 1 since
45	    /// the histogram's lower bound is 1 ns; sub-ns deltas from
46	    /// coarse timers round up rather than panic.
47	    pub fn record(&mut self, ns: u64) {
48	        self.hist.record(ns.max(1)).unwrap();
49	    }
50	
51	    /// Render a band-table report for this probe, indented one
52	    /// level deeper than the enclosing bench's report. No
53	    /// `adjusted` column — probe overhead subtraction is a
54	    /// separate concern.
55	    pub fn report(&self) {
56	        let sample_count = self.hist.len();
57	        println!(
58	            "  probe: {} [count={}]",
59	            self.name,
60	            fmt_commas(sample_count)
61	        );
62	        if sample_count == 0 {
63	            println!();
64	            return;
65	        }
66	
67	        let n_bands = BOUNDARY_PCTS.len() - 1;
68	        let mut band_first = vec![u64::MAX; n_bands];
69	        let mut band_last = vec![0u64; n_bands];
70	        let mut band_count = vec![0u64; n_bands];
71	        let mut band_sum = vec![0u128; n_bands];
72	
73	        let mut cumulative = 0u64;
74	        for iv in self.hist.iter_recorded() {
75	            let value = iv.value_iterated_to();
76	            let count = iv.count_at_value();
77	            let mid_rank = (cumulative as f64 + count as f64 / 2.0) / sample_count as f64;
78	            let idx = BOUNDARY_PCTS[1..]
79	                .iter()
80	                .position(|&b| mid_rank < b)
81	                .unwrap_or(n_bands - 1);
82	            band_first[idx] = band_first[idx].min(value);
83	            band_last[idx] = band_last[idx].max(value);
84	            band_count[idx] += count;
85	            band_sum[idx] += value as u128 * count as u128;
86	            cumulative += count;
87	        }
88	
89	        struct BandRow {
90	            label: String,
91	            first: String,
92	            last: String,
93	            range: String,
94	            count: String,
95	            mean: String,
96	        }
97	
98	        let mut rows: Vec<BandRow> = Vec::new();
99	        for i in 0..n_bands {
100	            if band_count[i] == 0 {
101	                continue;
102	            }
103	            let mean_val = band_sum[i] as f64 / band_count[i] as f64;
104	            rows.push(BandRow {
105	                label: format!("{}-{}", BOUNDARY_NAMES[i], BOUNDARY_NAMES[i + 1]),
106	                first: fmt_commas(band_first[i]),
107	                last: fmt_commas(band_last[i]),
108	                range: fmt_commas(band_last[i] - band_first[i] + 1),
109	                count: fmt_commas(band_count[i]),
110	                mean: fmt_commas_f64(mean_val, 0),
111	            });
112	        }
113	
114	        let label_w = rows
115	            .iter()
116	            .map(|r| r.label.len())
117	            .max()
118	            .unwrap_or(0)
119	            .max("stdev min-p99".len());
120	        let first_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.first.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);
121	        let last_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.last.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);
122	        let range_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.range.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);
123	        let count_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.count.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);
124	        let mean_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.mean.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);
125	
126	        const INDENT: &str = "    ";
127	        const GAP: &str = "    ";
128	
129	        let first_col = INDENT.len() + label_w + 1 + first_w;
130	        let last_gap = " ns".len() + GAP.len() + last_w;
131	        let range_gap = " ns".len() + GAP.len() + range_w;
132	        let count_gap = " ns".len() + GAP.len() + count_w;
133	        let mean_gap = GAP.len() + mean_w;
134	        println!(
135	            "{:>first_col$}{:>last_gap$}{:>range_gap$}{:>count_gap$}{:>mean_gap$}",
136	            "first", "last", "range", "count", "mean",
137	        );
138	
139	        for r in &rows {
140	            println!(
141	                "{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>first_w$} ns{GAP}{:>last_w$} ns{GAP}{:>range_w$} ns{GAP}{:>count_w$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns",
142	                r.label, r.first, r.last, r.range, r.count, r.mean,
143	            );
144	        }
145	
146	        let hist_mean = self.hist.mean();
147	        let skip = first_w
148	            + " ns".len()
149	            + GAP.len()
150	            + last_w
151	            + " ns".len()
152	            + GAP.len()
153	            + range_w
154	            + " ns".len()
155	            + GAP.len()
156	            + count_w;
157	        println!(
158	            "{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>skip$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns",
159	            "mean",
160	            "",
161	            fmt_commas_f64(hist_mean, 0),
162	        );
163	        println!(
164	            "{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>skip$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns",
165	            "stdev",
166	            "",
167	            fmt_commas_f64(self.hist.stdev(), 0),
168	        );
169	
170	        let trim_count: u64 = band_count[..n_bands - 1].iter().sum();
171	        if trim_count > 0 {
172	            let trim_sum: u128 = band_sum[..n_bands - 1].iter().sum();
173	            let trim_mean = trim_sum as f64 / trim_count as f64;
174	
175	            let mut trim_var_sum = 0.0f64;
176	            let mut trim_var_count = 0u64;
177	            let mut cum = 0u64;
178	            for iv in self.hist.iter_recorded() {
179	                let value = iv.value_iterated_to();
180	                let count = iv.count_at_value();
181	                let mid_rank = (cum as f64 + count as f64 / 2.0) / sample_count as f64;
182	                let idx = BOUNDARY_PCTS[1..]
183	                    .iter()
184	                    .position(|&b| mid_rank < b)
185	                    .unwrap_or(n_bands - 1);
186	                if idx < n_bands - 1 {
187	                    let diff = value as f64 - trim_mean;
188	                    trim_var_sum += diff * diff * count as f64;
189	                    trim_var_count += count;
190	                }
191	                cum += count;
192	            }
193	            let trim_stdev = if trim_var_count > 1 {
194	                (trim_var_sum / trim_var_count as f64).sqrt()
195	            } else {
196	                0.0
197	            };
198	
199	            println!(
200	                "{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>skip$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns",
201	                "mean min-p99",
202	                "",
203	                fmt_commas_f64(trim_mean, 0),
204	            );
205	            println!(
206	                "{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>skip$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns",
207	                "stdev min-p99",
208	                "",
209	                fmt_commas_f64(trim_stdev, 0),
210	            );
211	        }
212	        println!();
213	    }
214	}
215	
```

> AGENT

This is the same bug in the sibling report path (`probe.rs::Probe::report`), which the `tp`/`tp2` benches use — its trim label is hardcoded `min-p99` (probe.rs:201,207) and its rows are `low-high` ranges (`min-p1` … `p90-p99`). Same failure mode: if `min-p1` is empty (sparse data) or `p90-p99` is empty, `min-p99` names rows that aren't there.

**My recommendation: yes, fix now, in this same commit.** It's the identical fix in the parallel path, and the cycle's theme ("trim label spans populated bands") covers it cleanly — leaving `tp` on the old label would ship a visibly inconsistent tool. I'll adapt it to probe's `low-high` convention (so `min-p99` stays `min-p99` in the normal case, becomes `p10-p99` / `min-p90` at the empty ends).

415	central tendency and spread:

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/src/probe.rs",
  "old_string": "const BOUNDARY_NAMES: &[&str] = &[\n    \"min\", \"p1\", \"p10\", \"p20\", \"p30\", \"p40\", \"p50\", \"p60\", \"p70\", \"p80\", \"p90\", \"p99\", \"max\",\n];",
  "new_string": "const BOUNDARY_NAMES: &[&str] = &[\n    \"min\", \"p1\", \"p10\", \"p20\", \"p30\", \"p40\", \"p50\", \"p60\", \"p70\", \"p80\", \"p90\", \"p99\", \"max\",\n];\n\n/// Trimmed-stat range label from the populated bands below the\n/// `p99-max` tail (`band_count`'s last band).\n///\n/// - Names the first..last populated non-tail band low-to-high like\n///   the rows (`min-p99`, or `p10-p99` / `min-p90` when an end band\n///   is empty), so the label tracks the real extent instead of a\n///   fixed `min-p99` whose `min`/`p99` edges may not be present.\n/// - Empty string when no non-tail band is populated (the caller\n///   guards this with `trim_count > 0`, so the label goes unused).\nfn trim_range_label(band_count: &[u64]) -> String {\n    let trim_end = band_count.len() - 1; // exclude the p99-max tail band\n    let first = (0..trim_end).find(|&i| band_count[i] > 0);\n    let last = (0..trim_end).rev().find(|&i| band_count[i] > 0);\n    match (first, last) {\n        (Some(f), Some(l)) => format!(\"{}-{}\", BOUNDARY_NAMES[f], BOUNDARY_NAMES[l + 1]),\n        _ => String::new(),\n    }\n}"
}

150	### Config file
151	
152	Defaults and named pin profiles can live in a TOML config file, so
153	common invocations don't repeat flags. Precedence, lowest to
154	highest:
155	
156	- **built-in defaults** — `duration=5.0`, `band_labels=both`,
157	  `decimals=1`;
158	- **XDG file** — `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/iiac-perf/config.toml`, or
159	  `$HOME/.config/iiac-perf/config.toml` when `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` is
160	  unset; the per-user home for defaults and profiles;
161	- **project-local file** — `iiac-perf.toml` in the current
162	  directory (no upward walk); overrides the XDG file field by
163	  field, profiles merging by key;
164	- **CLI flags** — always win.
165	
166	The startup banner's `config` line names the files that were
167	loaded (or `none (built-in defaults)`). A present-but-malformed
168	file is a hard error rather than a silent fallback, so a typo
169	surfaces. Every key is optional;
170	[`iiac-perf.toml.example`](iiac-perf.toml.example) is a ready-to-copy
171	sample documenting each key and its possible values:
172	
173	```toml
174	duration     = 10.0     # default -d seconds
175	band_labels  = "zpn"    # zpn | frac | both
176	decimals     = 2        # 0-3
177	
178	[profiles]              # named --pin core specs
179	smt = "0,12"           # SMT siblings of one physical core (contention)
180	ccx = "0,1"            # independent cores, same CCX (best channel latency)
181	ccd = "0,6"            # cross-CCD
182	```
183	
184	Each bench prints a band-based histogram in nanoseconds. Each row
185	is one band, labeled by its **upper boundary**, the lower boundary
186	being the previous printed row: deciles in the body (`p10` …
187	`p90`) and **nines/zeros** notation in both tails, where `nK`/`zK`
188	mark the boundary with a fraction 10<sup>-K</sup> of samples above
189	(`n`) or below (`z`) it — so `n2` ≡ p99, `n3` ≡ p99.9, … `n10`,
190	and `z2` ≡ p1, `z3` ≡ p0.1, `z4`. "K nines" is standard
191	engineering shorthand for proportions near one
192	([Nines (notation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nines_%28notation%29),
193	nines = −log₁₀(1−x)); `zK` is this project's mirror of it for the
194	fast tail (the underlying concept is the
195	[survival function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Survival_function)
196	/ CCDF tail fraction). The slow tail subdivides down to `n10`, the
197	fast tail only to `z4` — a latency distribution is floored below
198	(nothing beats the fast path) and open above. A band only prints
199	when it has samples, so deep tail rows appear as run length earns
```
